Figure 5.2
:
Relationships between the Riemannian
f(\mathbf{G},\mathbb{B},\mathbf{B})
class of theories. The most general setup is
f(\mathbf{G},\mathbb{B},\mathbf{B})
, from which we obtain
f(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B})
or
f(\mathfrak{G},\mathfrak{B})
in the metric and tetradic representations respectively. The former is locally Lorentz invariant while the latter is diffeomorphism invariant.
Covariantisation of these models leads to the modified symmetric and metric teleparallel theories. Considering only the bulk terms leads to the second-order modifications. From
f(\mathbf{G})
and
f(\mathfrak{G})
we obtain GR by requiring invariance under diffeomorphisms and local Lorentz transformations.

As we have discussed, covariantisation of these theories gives rise to the teleparallel modifications. Specifically, we retrieve
f(Q,B_{Q})
gravity from the
f(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B})
theories, and
f(T,B_{T})
from the
f(\mathfrak{G},\mathfrak{B})
theories. Despite the teleparallel theories being fully covariant, the
f(R)
limit still represents a special case possessing an ‘enhanced’ symmetry: invariance under coordinate transformations of the metric tensor and local Lorentz transformations of the tetrad alone (without considering the affine connection). This is also special because the degrees of freedom in
f(R)
gravity are fewer than in the modified teleparallel theories
[
391
]
. This can be understood as the (dynamical) fields parameterising the teleparallel connection dropping out from the action entirely in the
f(R)
limit. We then are led to GR in the second-order, fully invariant limit, as expected.

On the other hand, if one wanted to work in the other direction, one can begin in the teleparallel framework and simply fix the gauge using the formulae developed in this work. This could be accomplished by introducing Lagrange multipliers to the teleparallel actions. We will briefly comment on this approach in the next section, when working in the metric-affine geometries.

We should also point out the similar modifications in general teleparallel gravity
[
160
,
333
]
, described by
f(\mathring{G})
. There,
\mathring{G}
represents the quadratic contortion terms of a teleparallel geometry with both torsion and non-metricity. This is the generalisation of the teleparallel action given in (
3.3.8
). Clearly this is closely related to our non-covariant theory here. However, the contractions of the contortion tensor include torsion and non-metricity cross terms, which are not immediately present in our
f(\mathbf{G},\mathbb{B},\mathbf{B})
model. The simplest realisation of this theory into our non-covariant scheme would be to work in a general anholonomic frame which is not orthonormal
[
373
]
. Then, the gauge-fixed theories would correspond to the vanishing affine connection in the mixed basis. However, this is beyond the scope of this thesis.

This unified framework also serves as a useful tool to compare and contrast the differences between the metric teleparallel and the symmetric teleparallel theories. It is curious that the equations of motion for these theories often coincide, leading to equivalent solutions
13
13

13

Of course, when perturbations are taken into account, metric vs tetrad perturbations may lead to differences.
. The case of FLRW cosmology is well-known and well-studied, and it can be attributed to the high amount of symmetry that these spacetimes possess. The same also occurs in spherically symmetric spacetimes, which we first pointed out in
[
222
]
, and has later been studied in more detail
[
413
]
.

Clearly a necessary condition for the equivalence of these theories is for the boundary term
\mathbb{B}=\mathfrak{G}-\mathbf{G}
to vanish. As we noted,
\mathbb{B}
is neither a coordinate scalar nor a Lorentz scalar, hence depends on both the coordinates and frames. One would hope that studying a theory of the form
f(\mathbf{G}+\alpha\mathbb{B})
, and using the additional constraints that come from diffeomorphism invariance and local Lorentz invariance, would be useful in further investigating the relationship between the
f(\mathbf{G})
and
f(\mathfrak{G})
theories. It would be interesting to prove (or disprove) whether a background solution of
f(Q)
gravity is always a solution of
f(T)
gravity, and vice-versa. Our gauge-fixed theories seem to be a useful approach for these types of questions, where we only have the metric and tetrad fields to deal with.

5.3
Metric-affine generalisations

Here we return to the metric-affine setting with an independent metric tensor and affine connection. This allows us to make contact with the other metric-affine theories directly, such as Einstein-Cartan theory. In particular, beginning with the affine decomposition studied in Sec.
4.3
, we will assume that the total curvature
\bar{R}_{\mu\nu\gamma}{}^{\lambda}
is non-vanishing. To set either non-metricity or torsion to zero, we will use the Lagrange multiplier method employed throughout the previous chapters.

Setting both torsion and non-metricity to zero will return to the Levi-Civita framework. But we will see that in some cases, such as when non-linear functions of boundary terms are considered, the modified theories do not reduce to their metric counterparts studied in the previous sections. This will be explained in more detail below.

5.3.1
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})
gravity

Let us now study the modifications of the metric-affine decomposition of the Ricci scalar
\bar{R}=\bar{\mathbf{G}}+\bar{\mathbf{B}}
, where we remind the reader that
\bar{\mathbf{G}}
and
\bar{\mathbf{B}}
are defined as

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{G}}

\displaystyle:=g^{\mu\lambda}\big(\bar{\Gamma}^{\kappa}_{\kappa\rho}\bar{\Gamma}^{\rho}_{\mu\lambda}-\bar{\Gamma}^{\kappa}_{\mu\rho}\bar{\Gamma}^{\rho}_{\kappa\lambda}\big)+\big(\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\mu\lambda}\delta^{\nu}_{\kappa}-\bar{\Gamma}^{\nu}_{\kappa\lambda}\big)\big(\partial_{\nu}g^{\kappa\lambda}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\alpha\beta}g^{\kappa\lambda}\partial_{\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{\bar{B}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\kappa}\big(\sqrt{-g}(g^{\mu\lambda}\bar{\Gamma}^{\kappa}_{\mu\lambda}-g^{\kappa\lambda}\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\mu\lambda})\big)\,.

Recall that all terms with a bar now refer to their metric-affine versions, and we work in the natural coordinate basis.

We propose the modified gravitational action to be an arbitrary function of the affine bulk and boundary terms
[
173
]

\displaystyle S_{\textrm{ grav}}[g,\bar{\Gamma}]=\frac{1}{2\kappa}\int f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})\sqrt{-g}\,d^{4}x\,.

(5.3.1)

Variations with respect to the independent metric and connection lead to

\delta S_{\textrm{grav}}=\frac{1}{2\kappa}\int\biggl\{\delta g^{\mu\nu}\Big[-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f+f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}\big(\bar{G}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\bar{\mathbf{G}}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\bar{E}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}\\
+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}\bar{\mathbf{B}}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{E}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}\Big]+\delta\bar{\Gamma}{}^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\Big[P^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}+2\partial_{\sigma}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}\delta_{\lambda}^{[\mu}g^{\sigma]\nu}\Big]\biggr\}\sqrt{-g}\,d^{4}x\,,

(5.3.2)

where the superpotential
\bar{E}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\lambda}
is defined in (
4.4.11
). For the derivation of the field equations, see Appendix
C.2.2
. Let us also include the matter action
S_{\textrm{M}}[g,\bar{\Gamma},\varPhi^{A}]
, which we assume to be minimally coupled to gravity. The full field equations are

-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f+f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}\big(\bar{G}_{(\mu\nu)}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\bar{\mathbf{G}}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\bar{E}_{(\mu\nu)}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}(f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}-f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}})+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}\bar{\mathbf{B}}=\kappa{}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}\,,

(5.3.3)

P^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}+2\partial_{\rho}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}\delta_{\lambda}^{[\mu}g^{\rho]\nu}=2\kappa\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}\,,

(5.3.4)

where the metric (Hilbert) energy-momentum tensor is defined in (
3.2.1
) and the hypermomentum defined in (
3.2.3
).

In the case that the action depends only on the bulk term
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}})
, all
f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}
terms vanish. Then, the connection equation is proportional to Palatini tensor
P^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}
(
4.4.15
). Recall that
\bar{\mathbf{G}}
is also algebraic in torsion and non-metricity (
4.4.8
), therefore in the
f(\mathbf{G})
theories the connection field equation is only algebraic. It is well known that in Einstein-Cartan theory, torsion does not propagate, which implies the absence of torsional gravitational waves in vacuum. This is in stark contrast to metric perturbations, which can propagate through vacuum regions of spacetime.
In order to obtain dynamical behaviour in either of these geometric quantities, we must include the boundary term
\bar{\mathbf{B}}
. It is precisely those boundary terms that contain the derivatives of torsion and non-metricity.

It is interesting to note that making the Palatini variations of
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}})
and then choosing the Levi-Civita connection (consistent with solving the connection field equation with vanishing hypermomentum) leads back to the metric
f(\mathbf{G})
gravity theory (
5.1.18
). However, performing the same procedure with
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})
does not yield the metric
f(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B})
model. This is perhaps unsurprising as the same situation occurs in Palatini
f(R)
gravity
[
306
,
92
,
93
]
. The reason is clear from the form of (
5.3.2
): if one assumes the Levi-Civita connection, the Palatini tensor
P^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}
vanishes identically. Therefore none of the content of the
f(\mathbf{G})
model is lost in the connection equations of motion. In contrast, the fourth-order terms coming from the
f(\ldots,\mathbf{B})
field equation are lost in the connection equation of motion. Specifically, the Levi-Civita connection includes derivatives of the metric, which means an integration by parts would be performed on the
\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\mathbf{B}}
term, leading to the fourth-order terms in the Levi-Civita variation of the
f(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B})
action.

In order to make direct contact with the teleparallel theories, one would need to consider a more general action than (
5.3.1
). Firstly, looking back at Fig.
5.2
, one can imagine a slightly more general setting with
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\mathbb{B},\bar{\mathbf{B}})
, where
\mathbb{B}
relates the coordinate basis and orthonormal basis bulk and boundary terms. This extension follows readily from the work in this chapter and the previous one. In the Levi-Civita limit, we would obtain all of Fig.
5.2
. Decomposing the affine quantities into their different geometric parts (
4.4.52
) yields an even more general type of theory
f(\mathbf{G},T,Q,\mathbf{C},\mathbb{B},\mathbf{B},B_{T},B_{Q})
, where again
\mathbb{B}
is used to relate the coordinate and orthonormal bases. Teleparallel theories are then directly included in this action. Moreover, one easily obtains the limit
f(\mathbf{G}+T+Q+\mathbf{C},\mathbf{B}-B_{T}+B_{Q})=f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})
.
In this section we will continue to work the
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})
theories, but this discussion should show how more general theories can be constructed, and how these relate with one another.

5.3.2
Diffeomorphism and projective invariance

Let us again return to the topic of symmetry and invariance for our modified action.
In Sec.
4.4.2
it was shown that the affine bulk and boundary terms were projectively invariant under the transformation

\bar{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}\rightarrow\bar{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}+\delta^{\gamma}_{\beta}P_{\alpha}\,.

The same was the case for the affine Ricci scalar
\bar{R}
, and in Sec.
3.1.1
we showed that the consequence was that the Palatini tensor is trace-free
P^{\mu\nu}{}_{\nu}=0
. Similarly, our modified action
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})
is then invariant under projective transformations, and this is reflected in the connection equation also being trace-free over the same indices. This is easily verified

P^{\mu\nu}{}_{\nu}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}+2\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}g^{\nu[\lambda}\delta^{\mu]}_{\nu}=\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}g^{\mu\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}g^{\lambda\mu}=0\,.

(5.3.5)

The situation here is the same as in the standard metric-affine GR case, or in modifications such as metric-affine
f(\bar{R})
gravity. There is little else to say on this topic, as these modifications neither help nor hinder any issues associated with projective invariance.

Moving on to diffeomorphisms, the action transforms under an infinitesimal symmetry transformation as

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}S_{\textrm{grav}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\kappa}\int\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\big(\sqrt{-g}f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})\big)d^{4}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\kappa}\int\xi^{\lambda}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\Big[\sqrt{-g}M^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}\big(f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}-f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}\big)\Big]d^{4}x\,.

(5.3.6)

Here we have performed the same calculations shown explicitly in Sec.
5.1.1
, and discarded any surface terms. We have also made use of the formulae for the Lie derivatives of these affine terms, see equations (
4.4.33
)-(
4.4.34
). For arbitrary
\xi
, this leaves the expression

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\Big[\sqrt{-g}M^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}\big(f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}-f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}\big)\Big]\,.

(5.3.7)

This expression could also be easily obtained by using the decompositions of the affine objects

\bar{\mathbf{G}}=\mathbf{G}+T+Q+C\,,\qquad\bar{\mathbf{B}}=\mathbf{B}-B_{T}+B_{Q}\,,

which we gave in equations (
4.4.53
) and (
4.4.54
). All terms on the right-hand side, except the bulk and boundary terms, are coordinate scalars. Hence, under a diffeomorphism, it is only the
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{B}
term that transforms non-covariantly, which directly leads to the conservation equation (
5.3.7
).

Unlike for an invariant scalar action, the above transformation need not vanish identically. The expression is also non-covariant and depends on the choice of coordinates, due to the non-tensorial nature of the bulk and boundary terms. One immediately sees that for the choice of function
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}}+\bar{\mathbf{B}})=f(\bar{R})
the expression vanishes, reflecting the diffeomorphism invariance of the Palatini
f(\bar{R})
theories.

If we insist that our gravitational action remain invariant under the above transformations, we obtain the conservation law

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\Big[\sqrt{-g}M^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}\big(f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}-f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}\big)\Big]=0\,.

(5.3.8)

We discussed this topic in detail in Sec.
5.1.1
, so we refer to that section for further analysis.
If we also include minimally coupled matter in our total action, which we remind the reader depends on the metric and the connection, we have the following (on-shell) conservation law

\frac{1}{2\kappa}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\Big[\sqrt{-g}M^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}\big(f_{,\bar{\mathbf{G}}}-f_{,\bar{\mathbf{B}}}\big)\Big]-\sqrt{-g}\nabla_{\mu}{}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T^{\mu}{}_{\lambda}\\
-\bar{\nabla}_{\nu}\bar{\nabla}_{\mu}(\sqrt{-g}\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda})-\sqrt{-g}\bar{R}_{\lambda\mu\nu}{}^{\rho}\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho}-\bar{\nabla}_{\mu}(\sqrt{-g}\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho})T^{\rho}{}_{\nu\lambda}=0\,.

(5.3.9)

For a consistent variation principle of the total action
S_{\textrm{total}}=S_{\textrm{grav}}+S_{\textrm{M}}
we require the above equation (
5.3.9
) to be satisfied. This is the most general case, as we do not consider non-minimal couplings here. One sees that the equation (
5.3.9
) reduces to the metric
f(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B})
conservation law given in equation (
5.1.13
) when the connection is Levi-Civita and hypermomentum is zero.

If the matter action
S_{\textrm{M}}
is a coordinate scalar then we must impose that the first of these equations (
5.3.8
) is satisfied. In that case, one obtains the usual metric-affine matter-hypermomentum conservation laws

\sqrt{-g}\nabla_{\mu}{}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T^{\mu}{}_{\lambda}+\bar{\nabla}_{\nu}\bar{\nabla}_{\mu}(\sqrt{-g}\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda})+\sqrt{-g}\bar{R}_{\lambda\mu\nu}{}^{\rho}\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho}\\
+\bar{\nabla}_{\mu}(\sqrt{-g}\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho})T^{\rho}{}_{\nu\lambda}=0\,,

(5.3.10)

see for example
[
148
]
. Vanishing hypermomentum leads to the standard energy-momentum conservation law
\nabla_{\mu}T^{\mu}{}_{\lambda}=0
. In Chapter
6
, when working in the cosmological setting, we will work with the standard perfect fluid matter representations, where hypermomentum vanishes and the matter action is a scalar. We will see that this forces the choice of coordinates to satisfy the gravitational conservation constraint (
5.3.8
).

In our work
[
173
]
, where we first proposed this theory, we conjectured on the covariantisation of the
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})
models. Our main questions were the following: how would such a procedure be implemented, and what would the resulting theory look like? We now feel to be in a better position to answer such questions. In Section
4.3
we showed how the Stueckelberg covariantisation could be applied to the Levi-Civita bulk and boundary terms in both the coordinate and orthonormal bases. Importantly, this procedure made no reference to geometry, even though it turned out that the compensating Stueckelberg fields could be given a geometric interpretation. It follows that the same procedure could be applied here, i.e., using the decomposition of
\bar{\mathbf{G}}
into
\mathbf{G}
and the other affine scalars (
4.4.53
), and then applying the Stueckelberg trick to the (metric) bulk part
\mathbf{G}
. This appears to give a satisfactory answer to the first question.

With regards to the second question, on the interpretation of the resulting theory, we must note that because the connection is not flat
\bar{R}_{\mu\nu\lambda}{}^{\gamma}\neq 0
, the Stueckelberg fields cannot be related to the affine connection of the spacetime. In other words, we do not obtain a neat geometric picture like we did when performing the covariantisation of the metric
f(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B})
theories.

However, one answer to this second question is that they could be related to a
different
, independent teleparallel connection. This seems reminiscent of the modified theories with two independent connections
[
414
]
, as well as the so-called ‘hybrid-Palatini’ theories
[
415
,
416
]
. In our case, the second (auxiliary) connection will be in terms of the Stueckelberg fields, taking the form of the ‘purely gauge’ symmetric teleparallel connection (
3.3.21
). This was actually shown to be consistent by Milgrom (in the context of the bimetric MOND theories
[
383
]
), where the connection associated with the auxiliary metric was fixed to be the symmetric teleparallel one
[
218
]
. This led to the covariant
f(Q)
theories. A similar approach was used for the non-covariant Einstein action by introducing an additional reference connection and metric
[
359
]
.
This would be an interesting avenue to explore further in the metric-affine context, which we believe has not been looked at before.

5.3.3
Equivalence with metric-affine
f(R)
gravity

Metric-affine
f(\bar{R})
gravity is of course also included in our approach and achieved by simply setting
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})=f(\bar{\mathbf{G}}+\bar{\mathbf{B}})
. This again follows from the decomposition of the affine Ricci scalar (
4.4.7
).
For the metric field equations (
5.3.3
), the
\bar{E}
terms cancel to give

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f+f^{\prime}(R)\bigl(\bar{G}_{(\mu\nu)}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\bar{\mathbf{G}}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\bar{\mathbf{B}}\bigr)=\kappa{}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}\,,

(5.3.11)

which can be written as

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\bar{R})\bar{R}_{(\mu\nu)}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f(\bar{R})=\kappa{}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}\,,

(5.3.12)

where we have used the relations
\bar{G}_{\mu\nu}+g_{\mu\nu}\bar{R}/2=\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}
and
\bar{\mathbf{G}}+\bar{\mathbf{B}}=\bar{R}
. The connection field equation is

\displaystyle P^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}f^{\prime}(R)+2\partial_{\rho}f^{\prime}(\bar{R})\delta_{\lambda}^{[\mu}g^{\rho]\nu}=2\kappa\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}\,.

(5.3.13)

This can be written in a more familiar form by noting that

\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\bar{\nabla}_{\rho}(f^{\prime}(R)\sqrt{-g}\delta_{\lambda}^{[\mu}g^{\rho]\nu})=2\partial_{\rho}f^{\prime}(\bar{R})\delta_{\lambda}^{[\mu}g^{\rho]\nu}\\
+f^{\prime}(\bar{R})\Big(\frac{1}{2}Q_{\lambda}{}^{\rho}{}_{\rho}g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Q^{\nu\rho}{}_{\rho}\delta^{\mu}_{\lambda}+Q_{\rho}{}^{\rho\nu}\delta^{\mu}_{\lambda}-Q_{\lambda}{}^{\mu\nu}\Big)\\
=2\partial_{\rho}f^{\prime}(\bar{R})\delta_{\lambda}^{[\mu}g^{\rho]\nu}+f^{\prime}(\bar{R})P^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}-f^{\prime}(\bar{R})(T^{\mu}{}_{\lambda}{}^{\nu}+g^{\mu\nu}T^{\rho}{}_{\rho\lambda}+\delta^{\mu}_{\lambda}T^{\rho\nu}{}_{\rho})\,,

(5.3.14)

where in the first line we expanded out the covariant derivatives of the metric in terms of the non-metricity tensor, and in the second line we inserted the definition of
P^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}
. Putting together (
5.3.13
) and (
5.3.14
) we arrive at

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\bar{\nabla}_{\rho}(f^{\prime}(R)\sqrt{-g}\delta_{\lambda}^{[\mu}g^{\rho]\nu})+f^{\prime}(\bar{R})(T^{\mu}{}_{\lambda}{}^{\nu}+g^{\mu\nu}T^{\rho}{}_{\rho\lambda}+\delta^{\mu}_{\lambda}T^{\rho\nu}{}_{\rho})=2\kappa\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\lambda}\,.

(5.3.15)

This is the familiar form of the
f(\bar{R})
connection equation of motion
[
306
]
.

In the case that matter does not couple to the connection,
à la
Palatini, it is known that this theory is equivalent to the scalar-tensor theory of Brans-Dicke (
1.2.10
) with parameter
\omega=-3/2

[
156
]
. Interestingly, this differs from its metric
f(R)
counterpart with
\omega=0
. As discussed earlier, this inequivalence is related to the boundary terms present in the affine Ricci scalar.

Taking the trace of the metric equation (
5.3.12
) leads to the algebraic relation
-2f(\bar{R})+f^{\prime}(\bar{R})\bar{R}=\kappa T
. This implies an algebraic relation between the Ricci scalar
\bar{R}
and the trace of the energy-momentum tensor
T

[
156
]
. Contrast this with the
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})
equations (
5.3.3
), where the trace leads to a dynamical equation as opposed to an algebraic one. This leads to a number of interesting possibilities beyond what can be found in the metric-affine
f(\bar{R})
theories.

5.3.4
Modified Einstein-Cartan gravity

Einstein-Cartan theory is derived in the metric-affine setting by imposing metric-compatibility of the connection. This was achieved in Sec.
3.2.2
by using the Lagrange multiplier action (
3.2.13
). Once the Lagrange multiplier was eliminated from the equations of motion, they read

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{(\mu\nu)}

\displaystyle=\kappa{}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}+\kappa(\tilde{\nabla}_{\rho}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\rho\sigma})\Delta^{\rho}{}_{(\mu\nu)}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{\mu\nu\rho}

\displaystyle=2\kappa\Delta_{\mu[\nu\rho]}\,,

see (
3.2.17
)-(
3.2.18
). Also recall that we were able to make use of geometric identities in order to rewrite the first field equation in such a way that the symmetry brackets could be removed

\tilde{G}_{\mu\nu}=\kappa{}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}+\kappa(\tilde{\nabla}_{\rho}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\rho\sigma})\Delta^{\rho}{}_{\nu\mu}\,,

Its symmetric part gives the original first field equation, whilst its skew part coincides with the second equation. Moreover, the right-hand side was shown to be equivalent with the canonical energy-momentum tensor (
3.2.21
).

Following our work
[
173
]
, and using the same Lagrange multiplier term

\displaystyle S_{Q}=-\int\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\mu\nu\rho}Q_{\mu\nu\rho}\sqrt{-g}\,d^{4}x=\int\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\mu\nu\rho}\bar{\nabla}_{\mu}g_{\nu\rho}\sqrt{-g}\,d^{4}x\,.

let us apply this to our modified theory. The complete modified metric field equation (
5.3.3
) now reads

-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f+f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\big(\tilde{G}_{(\mu\nu)}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{E}_{(\mu\nu)}{}^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\\
+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{E}_{(\mu\nu)}{}^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}+\kappa\tilde{\nabla}_{\sigma}\lambda^{\sigma}{}_{\mu\nu}+\kappa T^{\rho}{}_{\sigma\rho}\lambda^{\sigma}{}_{\mu\nu}=\kappa{}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}\,,

(5.3.16)

while the connection (
5.3.4
) and constraint equation are given by

\displaystyle\tilde{P}^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}+2\partial_{\sigma}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\delta_{\rho}^{[\mu}g^{\sigma]\nu}-2\kappa\lambda^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=2\kappa\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho}\,,

(5.3.17)

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}_{\mu}g_{\nu\rho}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(5.3.18)

Note that we have already implemented the constraint equation to remove any non-metricity terms which would otherwise be present in the field equations. For this reason, affine quantities appearing in the field equations now are denoted with a tilde, which implies non-metricity has been set to zero.

We will now follow the same method as shown for the standard Einstein-Cartan theory of eliminating the Lagrange multipliers and writing the equations in a unified way. We begin with the
f(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})
case, without boundary terms, and then look at the more general
f(\tilde{\mathbf{G}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}})
theories.

Modified Einstein-Cartan theory without boundary terms

Let us now return to the field equations (
5.3.16
)–(
5.3.18
) and assume
f(\tilde{\mathbf{G}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}})=f(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})
so that
f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}=0
. Moreover, we assume that non-metricity vanishes via the introduction of the Lagrange multiplier, in which case the field equations simplify to

-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f+f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\big(\tilde{G}_{(\mu\nu)}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{E}_{(\mu\nu)}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\\
+\kappa\tilde{\nabla}_{\rho}\lambda^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}+\kappa T^{\sigma}{}_{\rho\sigma}\lambda^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}=\kappa{}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}\,,

(5.3.19)

\tilde{P}^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}-2\kappa\lambda^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho}=2\kappa\Delta^{\mu\nu}{}_{\rho}\,.

(5.3.20)

As before, the Palatini tensor is now skew-symmetric in the last pair of indices and we find
-\lambda_{\mu\nu\rho}=\Delta_{\mu(\nu\rho)}
and
\tilde{P}_{\mu\nu\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}=2\kappa\Delta_{\mu[\nu\rho]}
. Hence, we can write the first and second field equations without any Lagrange multipliers in the form

-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f+f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\big(\tilde{G}_{(\mu\nu)}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{E}_{(\mu\nu)}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\\
=\kappa\Bigl({}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}+(\tilde{\nabla}_{\rho}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\rho\sigma})\Delta^{\rho}{}_{(\nu\mu)}\Bigr)\,,

(5.3.21)

\tilde{P}_{\mu\nu\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}=2\kappa\Delta_{\mu[\nu\rho]}\,.

(5.3.22)

The right-hand side of equation (
5.3.21
) now takes the familiar form of the symmetrised canonical energy-momentum tensor.

We now wish to follow the same approach that we used in the standard Einstein-Cartan theory, of removing the symmetry brackets and considering the full equation instead. Taking the skew-symmetric part of (
5.3.21
), we find

\displaystyle f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\tilde{G}_{[\mu\nu]}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{E}_{[\mu\nu]}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}

\displaystyle=\kappa(\tilde{\nabla}_{\rho}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\rho\sigma})\Delta^{\rho}{}_{[\nu\mu]}

(5.3.23)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{\nabla}_{\rho}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\rho\sigma})(\tilde{P}^{\rho}{}_{\nu\mu}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}})\,.

(5.3.24)

After taking into account the geometric identity
\tilde{G}_{[\mu\nu]}=(\tilde{\nabla}_{\rho}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\rho\sigma})\tilde{P}^{\rho}{}_{\nu\mu}/2
we arrive at the interesting equation
14
14

14

Note that here we have somewhat abused notation by assuming that
f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}
acts as a scalar when acted on by the covariant derivative. This is of course not well defined. However, our purpose here is only to rewrite the field equations in a more suggestive form. We will use the original forms (
5.3.21
)-(
5.3.22
) when performing calculations.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tilde{E}_{[\mu\nu]}{}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{P}^{\rho}{}_{\nu\mu}\partial_{\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\,,

(5.3.25)

which immediately gives
\tilde{E}_{[\mu\nu]}{}^{\rho}=\tilde{P}^{\rho}{}_{\nu\mu}
. This equation can be seen as determining the skew-symmetric part of
\tilde{E}_{[\mu\nu]}{}^{\rho}
such that, in complete analogy with Einstein-Cartan theory, the full first field equation contains the second field equation. This allows us to define a new object as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho}

\displaystyle:=\tilde{E}_{(\mu\nu)}{}^{\rho}+\tilde{E}_{[\mu\nu]}{}^{\rho}

\displaystyle:=2\tilde{\Gamma}^{\rho}_{(\mu\nu)}-2\delta^{\rho}_{(\mu}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\lambda}_{|\lambda|\nu)}-g_{\mu\nu}(g^{\kappa\lambda}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\rho}_{\lambda\kappa}-g^{\kappa\rho}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\lambda}_{\lambda\kappa})+\tilde{P}^{\rho}{}_{\nu\mu}

\displaystyle=2\tilde{\Gamma}{}^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}+2\delta^{\rho}_{[\mu}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\lambda}_{\nu]\lambda}-2\delta^{\rho}_{\mu}\tilde{\Gamma}{}^{\lambda}_{\lambda\nu}-g_{\mu\nu}(g^{\kappa\lambda}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\rho}_{\lambda\kappa}-g^{\kappa\rho}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\lambda}_{\lambda\kappa})\,.

(5.3.26)

We are now able to re-write the complete first field equation

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f+f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\big(\tilde{G}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}=\kappa\Bigl({}^{(\tilde{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}+(\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\lambda\sigma})\Delta^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}\Bigr)\,,

(5.3.27)

so that its skew-symmetric part coincides with the second field equation. This result is somewhat surprising but illustrates the intricate relationship between the two field equations. It also reduces to standard Einstein-Cartan when
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}})=\bar{\mathbf{G}}
as expected.

Modified Einstein-Cartan theory with boundary terms

Let us now work with the full
f(\tilde{\mathbf{G}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}})
theory, given by equations (
5.3.16
)-(
5.3.18
). The connection equation with lowered indices is

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{\mu\nu\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}+2g_{\mu[\rho}\partial_{\nu]}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}-2\kappa\lambda_{\mu\nu\rho}=2\kappa\Delta_{\mu\nu\rho}\,,

(5.3.28)

from which we immediately see the symmetric part
\lambda_{\mu\nu\rho}=-\Delta_{\mu(\nu\rho)}
isolates the Lagrange multiplier, just like before. The skew-symmetric part now includes an additional dynamical term

\displaystyle P_{\mu\nu\rho}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}+2g_{\mu[\rho}\partial_{\nu]}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}=2\kappa\Delta_{\mu[\nu\rho]}\,.

(5.3.29)

The metric field equation is then

-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f+f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\big(\tilde{G}_{(\mu\nu)}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{E}_{(\mu\nu)}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}(f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}-f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}})+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\\
=\kappa\Big({}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}+(\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\lambda\sigma})\Delta^{\lambda}{}_{(\nu\mu)}\Big)\,.

(5.3.30)

Just as before, we now remove the symmetry brackets and consider the full field equation. First, the antisymmetric part can be written as

\displaystyle f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\tilde{G}_{[\mu\nu]}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{E}_{[\mu\nu]}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}(f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}-f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}})=\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\lambda\sigma})\Big(\tilde{P}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}+2\delta^{\lambda}_{[\mu}\partial_{\nu]}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\Big)\,,

(5.3.31)

where on the right-hand side we have used the skew-symmetric part of the connection equation. By again using the geometric identity
\tilde{G}_{[\mu\nu]}=(\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\lambda\sigma})\tilde{P}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}/2
we find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\Big(\tilde{E}_{[\mu\nu]}{}^{\lambda}-\tilde{P}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{E}_{[\mu\nu]}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}=(\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\lambda\sigma})\delta^{\lambda}_{[\mu}\partial_{\nu]}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\,.

(5.3.32)

The part in the first bracket was derived in the
f(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})
case (
5.3.25
). We can expand the right-hand side, cancelling the second partial derivatives, to obtain

\displaystyle(\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\lambda\sigma})\delta^{\lambda}_{[\mu}\partial_{\nu]}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(T^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\mu\sigma}\partial_{\nu}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}-T^{\sigma}{}_{\nu\sigma}\partial_{\mu}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\Big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{P}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\,,

(5.3.33)

where the first torsion term comes from commutator of covariant derivatives. Putting this back into our skew-symmetric field equation (
5.3.32
) we find

\displaystyle\Big(\tilde{E}_{[\mu\nu]}{}^{\lambda}-\tilde{P}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}\Big)\Big(\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}-\partial_{\lambda}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\Big)=0\,,

(5.3.34)

where we again have
\tilde{E}_{[\mu\nu]}{}^{\lambda}=\tilde{P}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}
. If we use the object
\mathcal{E}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\lambda}
introduced in (
5.3.26
), we can write the full field equation as

-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f+f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\big(\tilde{G}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}_{\mu\nu}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\big(f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}-f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\big)+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\\
=\kappa\Big({}^{(\bar{\Gamma})}T_{\mu\nu}+(\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}+T^{\sigma}{}_{\lambda\sigma})\Delta^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}\Big)\,.

(5.3.35)

The symmetric part contains the metric field equation (
5.3.30
) and the skew part coincides with the connection equation (
5.3.29
). Again, the right-hand side could be written using the canonical energy-momentum tensor.

The presence of the boundary terms in the form
f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}
in the connection field equation makes this model distinctly different to the previous modified Einstein-Cartan type theories. In equations (
3.2.18
) and (
5.3.22
), the vanishing of the source term
\Delta_{\mu[\nu\lambda]}=0
(which corresponds to the intrinsic spin of matter) implies the vanishing of the Palatini tensor. This in turn implies the vanishing of the torsion tensor, and one finds that torsion is non-dynamical. This is the case in standard Einstein-Cartan and the
f(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})
modifications.

Let us now set the intrinsic spin to zero
\Delta_{\mu[\nu\lambda]}=0
in our
f(\tilde{\mathbf{G}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}})
modifications (
5.3.29
). Using the expression for the Palatini tensor in terms of torsion, we can solve this equation for the torsion tensor

\displaystyle T^{\mu}{}_{\lambda\nu}=\frac{1}{f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}}\delta^{\mu}_{[\lambda}\partial_{\nu]}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\,.

(5.3.36)

Therefore, in general, torsion does not vanish in source-free regions of spacetime. It should be recalled that the boundary term itself contains contributions from torsion, which means that
\partial_{\mu}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}
contains (second) derivatives of the torsion tensor. Consequently, Eq. (
5.3.36
) is a partial differential equation for torsion. Using the chain rule we have

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}=f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}+f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\,,

(5.3.37)

so that our previous expression (
5.3.36
) can also be written as

\displaystyle T^{\mu}{}_{\lambda\nu}=\frac{f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}}{f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}}\delta^{\mu}_{[\lambda}\partial_{\nu]}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}+\frac{f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}}{f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}}\delta^{\mu}_{[\lambda}\partial_{\nu]}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\,.

(5.3.38)

Neglecting the peculiar situations in which the partial derivative terms vanish, we can make the following general statements. Firstly, the
f(\tilde{\mathbf{G}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}})
Einstein-Cartan type models have non-dynamical torsion if
f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}=\mathrm{const.}
, which corresponds to functions linear in the boundary term. In other words, this reduces to
f(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})
as we expected. Secondly, if
f_{,\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}\neq\mathrm{const.}
, torsion becomes dynamical and has the potential to propagate.

As was already remarked on various occasions, these boundary terms play a crucial role in these modified theories of gravity. Models depending on non-linear functions of these boundary terms show distinctly different properties than models with a linear term. In the next chapter we will study the cosmological applications of these modified boundary models in the Einstein-Cartan framework.

Also note that just like in all the theories studied in this chapter, equation (
5.3.38
) has the peculiar property of having some tensor quantities (here the torsion tensor) equated with non-covariant objects. This must be understood in the context of having
fixed
a system of coordinates, which is imposed by the conservation equations (
5.3.8
). Once coordinates have been fixed, these equations have a well-defined and proper meaning. We also discussed how all of these theories can be seen as gauge-fixed versions of fully covariant theories, which gives a legitimate interpretation of ‘fixed coordinates’. How this is implemented in practice will be seen explicitly in the following chapter.

Chapter 6
Cosmology

In this chapter we study the cosmological solutions for the modified theories introduced in Chapter
5
. We take for granted the standard cosmology of General Relativity as background knowledge, see
[
272
,
13
]
for reference. We also do not consider perturbations, working only at the background level. This makes the analysis fairly simple. For more detailed studies in relation to cosmological observations, such as Baryonic Acoustic Oscillations (BAO) data and CMB constraints, as well as gravitational wave propagation, we refer to the works
[
145
,
396
,
417
]
for
f(T)
gravity and
[
418
,
419
,
420
]
for
f(Q)
gravity. These results also apply to our
f(\mathbf{G})
and
f(\mathfrak{G})
theories by extension.

As demonstrated in the previous chapter, the dynamics of
f(\mathbf{G})
and
f(Q)
gravity and of
f(\mathfrak{G})
and
f(T)
gravity are completely equivalent. In the first section we explicitly show that this equivalence also extends to the background cosmological equations of
f(\mathbf{G})
and
f(\mathfrak{G})
gravity. As a concrete example, we then study the Born-Infeld Lagrangian in our
f(\mathbf{G})
models, which leads to early-time inflationary dynamics. This has also been reported in the
f(T)
case
[
381
]
.

In Sec.
6.2
we apply a dynamical systems approach to all of the second-order modified theories. This is again possible because the cosmological field equations for both
f(\mathbf{G})
and
f(\mathfrak{G})
coincide, provided we work in appropriate coordinates and tetrads. In our analysis, we focus on the particularly interesting solutions that describe accelerated (de Sitter) expansion. We derive a number of specific constraints that a model must satisfy in order for these solutions to exist. Importantly, these are model-independent results, making the analysis very broad. We then focus on the phase portraits for two recently proposed
f(Q)
models, comparing their qualitative behaviour to the standard
\Lambda
CDM cosmologies.

In the last section we study the
f(\bar{\mathbf{G}},\bar{\mathbf{B}})
theories, which depart from the teleparallel models by being genuine metric-affine theories. A simple quadratic boundary term model is chosen to study the cosmological dynamics in the Riemann-Cartan geometry. In this model, torsion is dynamical despite its source current (hypermomentum or intrinsic spin) being zero in cosmology. This propagating torsion field is found to give rise to early-time inflationary behaviour
[
173
]
.

6.1
Second-order modified cosmology

Here we study the cosmological solutions of the Riemannian second-order modified theories of the previous chapter, namely
f(\mathbf{G})
and
f(\mathfrak{G})
gravity. This of course extends to the
f(Q)
and
f(T)
theories via covariantisation. In our gauge-fixed theories, we must be careful that the choice of coordinates and tetrad lead to non-trivial dynamics, whilst also respecting the symmetries of the spacetime. This will be checked explicitly.

Throughout this chapter, we assume the validity of the cosmological principle, namely, that on sufficiently large scales the universe is homogeneous and isotropic
1
1

1

We do, however, note that there are good reasons to consider going beyond this assumption and studying models with anisotropy such as those of the Bianchi kind
[
421
]
.
. Consequently, we can model the universe using the Friedmann-Lemaître-Robertson-Walker (FLRW) line element. We also restrict our study to spatially flat models, in agreement with most current observational data
[
422
]
. This line element reads

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-N^{2}(t)dt^{2}+a^{2}(t)\big(dx^{2}+dy^{2}+dz^{2}\big)\,,

(6.1.1)

where
a(t)
is the scale factor and
N(t)
is the lapse function.

In some symmetry-breaking theories of modified gravity, it is not necessarily true that the lapse function can be set to one. On the other hand, in fully diffeomorphism invariant theories one can always rescale the time coordinate to absorb the lapse function into the new coordinates. However, in
f(\mathbf{G})
gravity for this metric (
6.1.1
), there exists the aforementioned time reparameterisation invariance
[
147
,
202
]
. We therefore see that we can indeed set
N(t)=1
. This is also the case in
f(\mathfrak{G})
gravity, where the full diffeomorphism symmetry under spacetime coordinate transformations is preserved.

We assume the energy-momentum tensor to be modelled by a single perfect fluid with pressure
p(t)
and energy density
\rho(t)
, taking the form

T^{\mu\nu}=(\rho+p)U^{\mu}U^{\nu}+pg^{\mu\nu}\ ,

(6.1.2)

where
U^{\mu}
is the normalised timelike four-velocity
U^{\mu}U_{\mu}=-1
.
We will later also assume a linear equation of state
p=w\rho
to obtain closed-form solutions. The
f(\mathbf{G})
cosmological field equations are then given by
[
222
]

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{G})N^{2}+12H^{2}f^{\prime}(\mathbf{G})

\displaystyle=2\kappa\rho\,,

(6.1.3)

\displaystyle-f(\mathbf{G})-f^{\prime}(\mathbf{G})\frac{4}{N^{2}}\Bigl(\dot{H}+3H^{2}-H\frac{\dot{N}}{N}\Bigr)+f^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{G})\frac{48H^{2}}{N^{4}}\Bigl(\dot{H}-H\frac{\dot{N}}{N}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=2\kappa p\,,

(6.1.4)

where
H=\dot{a}/a
is the Hubble function. In these coordinates
\mathbf{G}
is related to the Hubble parameter by

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}=-6\frac{H^{2}}{N^{2}}\,.

(6.1.5)

The field equations are second-order in derivatives of the metric, or first-order in
\{H(t),N(t)\}
. The first equation (
6.1.3
) is a generalised Friedmann constraint equation, containing no derivatives of the Hubble and lapse functions, while the second (
6.1.4
) is a modified acceleration equation.

These equations satisfy the usual continuity equation

\dot{\rho}+3H(\rho+p)=0\,,

(6.1.6)

which can be verified explicitly from (
6.1.3
) and (
6.1.4
). This can also be seen by noting that the conservation equation (
5.1.19
) vanishes for this choice of metric and coordinates, independent from the choice of
f
. If one works in the full
f(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B})
theory, the conservation equation (
5.1.14
) is also zero. This implies directly that the energy-momentum tensor is covariantly conserved
\nabla_{\mu}T^{\mu}{}_{\nu}=0
, see equation (
5.1.22
).

In the language of symmetric teleparallel gravity, we see that the Cartesian coordinates are consistent with the coincident gauge (zero affine connection). The vanishing of the
f(\mathbf{G})
conservation equation implies that the
f(Q)
connection equation of motion (
5.2.13
) also vanishes.
The non-metricity scalar then takes the form
Q=-\mathbf{G}=6H^{2}/N^{2}
, with the gauge boundary term being equal to zero
b_{\xi}=0
. Along with the appropriate field redefinitions, explained in Sec.
5.2.2
, the cosmological equations for
f(Q)
gravity in the coincident gauge are then simply (
6.1.3
)-(
6.1.4
).

Cosmological solutions in symmetric teleparallel gravity have been well studied, see for instance
[
202
]
. In
[
402
,
371
]
the authors also consider solutions beyond the coincidence gauge. This is especially useful when studying non-flat
k\neq 0
cosmologies
2
2

2

Note that
k\neq 0
can still be studied in the coincident gauge, but the coordinates for the metric tensor become extremely complicated
[
371
]
.
. We also note that for the
f(Q)
theories, the geometry is non-Riemannian and so extra care must be taken with regard to the coupling of matter. In particular, the role of hypermomentum can become important, which has been studied in this cosmological context
[
423
,
424
,
371
]
. In our
f(\mathbf{G})
framework, however, the geometry is Riemannian and no such considerations are needed.

Setting the lapse function to one, the field equations reduce to the simple form
[
223
]

\displaystyle 6f^{\prime}H^{2}+\frac{1}{2}f

\displaystyle=\kappa\rho\,,

(6.1.7)

\displaystyle(12H^{2}f^{\prime\prime}-f^{\prime})\dot{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{2}(\rho+p)\,,

(6.1.8)

with the bulk term given by
\mathbf{G}=-6H^{2}
. This can be seen to match the
f(Q)
field equations
[
202
]
, again making sure to take the correct redefinitions to match the various sign conventions throughout the literature. To be concrete and make contact with the cosmological equations shown in
[
202
,
371
]
, whose non-metricity scalar matches our conventions, we can make the redefinition
f(\mathbf{G})\rightarrow-f(-\mathbf{G})
and then apply the coincident gauge relation
-\mathbf{G}=Q
to obtain

\displaystyle 6f^{\prime}(Q)H^{2}-\frac{1}{2}f(Q)

\displaystyle=\kappa\rho\,,

(6.1.9)

\displaystyle\Big(12H^{2}f^{\prime\prime}(Q)+f^{\prime}(Q)\Big)\dot{H}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa}{2}(\rho+p)\,.

(6.1.10)

What is quite remarkable is that these equations are identical to the field equations of
f(T)
gravity
[
157
,
402
]
(or equivalently,
f(\mathfrak{G})
gravity).
To show this equivalence, we first must choose a frame field
\mathbf{e}_{a}
to work with. Due to the invariance of the metric under local Lorentz transformations, there is no unique choice for the tetrad leading to the FLRW metric (
6.1.1
). For the pure formulation of
f(T)
gravity or
f(\mathfrak{G})
gravity, we must ensure that our choice is compatible with the Weitzenböck gauge
[
152
]
. A ‘proper tetrad’ that satisfies this gauge choice
[
170
]
is given by

e^{a}{}_{\mu}={\textrm{diag}}\big(1,a(t),a(t),a(t)\big)\,,

(6.1.11)

which leads to the FLRW metric (
6.1.1
) with the lapse set to one. The tetradic bulk term is found to coincide with its metric counterpart
\mathfrak{G}=-6H(t)^{2}=\mathbf{G}
, and hence the torsion scalar also coincides with the non-metricity scalar
T=Q=6H(t)^{2}
.

The equivalence of the metric and tetradic bulk terms can be seen as a consequence of the vanishing of the boundary term
\mathbb{B}=\mathbf{G}-\mathfrak{G}
for this particular frame (
6.1.11
). Moreover, this choice of tetrad and metric can also be seen as a requirement imposed by the coincident and Weitzenböck gauges, or alternatively, the equations related to diffeomorphism and local Lorentz invariance. It is clear that further study of this boundary term
\mathbb{B}
is useful in relation to these discussions.

What is perhaps more surprising is that the superpotentials of both theories also coincide, leading to a complete equivalence between the
f(\mathbf{G})
and
f(\mathfrak{G})
cosmological field equations, see
[
222
,
223
]
. It then follows that equations (
6.1.7
)-(
6.1.8
) describe the background dynamics for both metric and tetradic theories, as well as the teleparallel
f(Q)
and
f(T)
theories (in their respective unitary gauges). We will therefore use these equations to study the cosmologies of all of these second-order modified theories.

6.1.1
Born-Infeld cosmology

As a brief example of cosmological dynamics beyond GR, let us look at the Born-Infeld Lagrangian (
5.1.20
) within the
f(\mathbf{G})
framework

L_{BI}=f(\mathbf{G})=\lambda\Bigg(\sqrt{1+\frac{2\mathbf{G}}{\lambda}}-1\Bigg)\,,

(6.1.12)

where we now have
\mathbf{G}=-6H^{2}
. The analysis largely follows the analogous
f(T)
case, see
[
381
]
.
We mentioned in Sec.
5.1.2
that these models can be constructed to break diffeomorphism invariance around some characteristic length scale
l\propto 1/\sqrt{|\lambda|}
. We retrieve GR in the limit that
|\lambda|\rightarrow\infty
and
l\rightarrow 0
. We are therefore interested in large but finite values of
\lambda
such that
l
is small but nonzero
3
3

3

Note that to make contact with the
f(Q)
and
f(T)
theories using our conventions, one can perform
\lambda\rightarrow-\lambda
, which is equivalent to
f(\mathbf{G})\rightarrow-f(-\mathbf{G})
.
.

For this action (
6.1.12
), the cosmological field equations can be written in the following form

\displaystyle\frac{6H(t)^{2}}{\sigma(t)+\sigma(t)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\rho(t)\,

(6.1.13)

\displaystyle\frac{6H(t)^{2}}{\sigma(t)+\sigma(t)^{2}}+\frac{2\dot{H}(t)}{\sigma(t)^{3}}

\displaystyle=-w\rho(t)\,,

(6.1.14)

where we assumed a barotropic equation of state
w=p/\rho
with
w>-1
and set
\kappa=1
. We have also introduced the convenient function
\sigma(t)
to simplify the field equations, defined by

\sigma(t):=\sqrt{1+\frac{2\mathbf{G}}{\lambda}}=\sqrt{1-\frac{12H(t)^{2}}{\lambda}}\,.

(6.1.15)

In the GR limit we simply have
\sigma(t)\rightarrow 1
, and one sees the cosmological equations (
6.1.13
)-(
6.1.14
) reduce to their usual form.

The field equations lead to the following differential equation for the Hubble factor

\dot{H}=\frac{1}{4}(1+w)(\lambda-12H^{2})(\sigma(t)-1)\,.

(6.1.16)

In the GR limit
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
the RHS simplifies to
-3H^{2}(1+w)/2
, yielding the standard solution for the scale factor
a(t)\propto(t-t_{0})^{2/(3(1+w))}
. Equation (
6.1.16
) yields analytic solutions, but it turns out to be easier to eliminate the Hubble function and work directly with the energy density.

From the field equations we find that the matter energy density
\rho(t)
satisfies the differential equation

\dot{\rho}=\pm\sqrt{3}(1+w)\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}\sqrt{\rho^{3}(\rho+\lambda)}}{\lambda+2\rho}\,,

(6.1.17)

where decreasing solutions
\dot{\rho}(t)<0
corresponding to an expanding universe
H(t)>0
and increasing solutions
\dot{\rho}(t)>0
corresponding to a contracting universe
H(t)<0
. Here we will focus on the expanding
H(t)>0
solutions.

In the limit
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
we obtain
\rho\propto 1/t^{2}
as expected. Somewhat surprisingly, equation (
6.1.17
) yields analytic solutions, without needing to make any series expansion for
\lambda
. This is in contrast to some previous results for Born-Infeld models in
f(T)
. For example, in
[
425
]
the authors study the
f(T)
Born-Infeld cosmological equations up to order
1/\lambda
. However, the results are qualitatively the same, and we refer to that work for further details.

Using the continuity equation (
6.1.6
) leads to the standard relationship between the energy density and the scale factor

\rho(t)=\rho_{0}a(t)^{-3(1+w)}\,,

(6.1.18)

where
\rho_{0}
is a constant related to the present-day energy density and scale factor. Using this with the solutions to the differential equation (
6.1.17
), we can find exact solutions for the scale factor. In Fig.
6.1
we plot these solutions for the scale factor
a(t)
for the values
\lambda=5
,
\lambda=100
and
\lambda=\infty
with equation of state
w=0
and
w=1/3
, corresponding to matter and radiation fluids respectively. The
\lambda=\infty
case is simply the GR solution with
a(t)\propto t^{2/(3+3w)}
. For large values of
t
the modified scale factor
a(t)
tracks the GR solution. However, for finite
\lambda
the initial time
t\rightarrow 0
singularity of GR is removed, with
a(t)
continuing smoothly in the negative
t
direction.

(a)

Solutions for scale factor
a(t)
with equation of state
w=0
. The
\lambda=\infty
solution corresponds to the GR limit with
a(t)\propto t^{2/3}
.

(b)

Solutions for scale factor
a(t)
with equation of state
w=1/3
. The
\lambda=\infty
solution corresponds to the GR limit with
a(t)\propto t^{1/2}
.

Figure 6.1
:
Scale factor solutions for the Born-Infeld action (
6.1.12
).

(a)

Hubble factor
H(t)
.

(b)

Ricci scalar
R

(c)

Ricci tensor squared
R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}
.

Figure 6.2
:
Hubble factor and curvature scalars for the Born-Infeld action (
6.1.12
). Solutions give rise to constant curvature regimes as
t\rightarrow-\infty
.

In fact, as
t\rightarrow-\infty
the modified scale factor behaves like the de Sitter solution

a(t)\approx\exp( \alpha t )\qquad,\qquad\alpha=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{2\sqrt{3}}\,.

(6.1.19)

The Hubble function therefore approaches the constant value
H(t)=\alpha
as
t\rightarrow-\infty
. This is shown in Fig.
6.2(a)
, where various values of
\lambda
are plotted against the GR solution. To see more clearly that these regions represent regimes of constant curvature, in Fig.
6.2(b)
and
6.2(c)
we also plot the curvature invariants for the modified expanding solutions

R=6\frac{\dot{a}^{2}+a\ddot{a}}{a^{2}}\,,\qquad R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}=3\frac{3a^{2}\ddot{a}^{2}+(2\dot{a}^{2}+a\ddot{a})^{2}}{a^{4}}\,.

(6.1.20)

We find that these two invariants take a maximum value of
R_{\textrm{max}}=\lambda
and
(R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu})_{\textrm{max}}=\lambda^{2}/4
in the limit
t\rightarrow-\infty
, as one would expect for the de Sitter solutions (
6.1.19
). They also remain finite for all
t
. These results are also reported in
[
381
,
382
]
for
f(T)
gravity, which verifies our solutions.

In conclusion, we find the Born-Infeld extensions are a particularly interesting class of models to study within these modified theories. In particular, they lead to geodesically complete spacetimes without an initial big bang singularity
[
381
]
. Instead, there is a geometrically sourced inflationary stage with de Sitter solutions for
t<0
. We will study similar solutions in the metric-affine setting in Sec.
6.3
. In the next section we focus on modifications that lead to
late-time
de Sitter solutions.

6.2
Dynamical systems approach

Let us analyse the cosmological field equations for the second-order modifications, given in (
6.1.7
)-(
6.1.8
). We can choose to use any of the pseudo-scalars
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathfrak{G}
or teleparallel scalars
Q
and
T
for this discussion, with appropriate redefinitions of the function
f
, due to the aforementioned equivalence

T=Q=-\mathbf{G}=-\mathfrak{G}=6H(t)^{2}\,.

(6.2.1)

In this section we will choose to use the non-metricity scalar
Q
.

At the background level, it makes sense to study all of these theories in a unified way. The theory of dynamical systems turns out to be extremely useful in this regard. We refer to the standard textbooks on dynamical systems
[
426
,
427
]
, and the textbooks
[
428
,
429
]
for applications in cosmology. For a recent review of applications in modified gravity, see
[
430
]
. Here we will follow our work
[
223
]
and outline some of the results of our analysis
4
4

4

Actually, our work
[
223
]
can be applied to
any
second-order system, provided the first constraint equation is some function of
H(t)
and the second equation is a function of
H(t)
and
\dot{H}(t)
.
. In Appendix
E
we include supplementary details for the dynamical systems analysis, referenced where necessary.

For a universe comprised of two fluids, matter
\rho_{m}
(
w=0
) and radiation
\rho_{r}
(
w=1/3
), the cosmological equations (
6.1.9
)-(
6.1.10
) can be written as

\displaystyle-\frac{f}{6H^{2}}-\frac{\rho_{m}}{3H^{2}}-\frac{\rho_{r}}{3H^{2}}+2f^{\prime}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(6.2.2)

\displaystyle\rho_{m}+\frac{4}{3}\rho_{r}+2\dot{H}(f^{\prime}+12H^{2}f^{\prime})

\displaystyle=0\,.

(6.2.3)

We now introduce the dynamical variables

\displaystyle X_{1}=\Omega_{m}=\frac{\rho_{m}}{3H^{2}}\,,\qquad X_{2}=\Omega_{r}=\frac{\rho_{r}}{3H^{2}}\,,\qquad Z=\frac{H^{2}}{H_{0}^{2}+H^{2}}\,.

(6.2.4)

The first two variables are the standard density parameters for matter and radiation
[
428
]
, whilst the
Z
is related to the Hubble function and chosen to make the phase space compact
[
431
]
. Note that the variables are independent from the function
f
, making this approach quite different from the usual dynamical systems studies
[
430
]
. It also follows that
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are always non-negative, and
0\leq Z\leq 1
.

The Friedmann constraint in terms of the new variables is

\displaystyle X_{1}+X_{2}=\big(1-\frac{1}{Z}\big)\frac{f}{6H_{0}^{2}}+2f^{\prime}\ ,

(6.2.5)

where we treat
f=f({6H_{0}^{2}Z}/{(1-Z)})
as a function of
Z
. Clearly the Friedmann constraint now depends on the form of
f
, which is the price to pay for using dynamical variables independent from the function
f
. Traditionally, additional variables are often introduced to completely eliminate
f
from the Friedmann constraint, at the cost of increasing the dimensionality of the system, e.g.,
[
432
,
202
]
. Our formulation has the advantage of having fewer variables with a lower dimensional phase space and is therefore computationally simpler to study. We provide a thorough discussion of this topic and a comparison of different approaches in
[
223
,
224
]
.

The three-dimensional phase space
\{X_{1},X_{2},Z\}
is reduced to two dimensions by using the Friedmann constraint (
6.2.5
). It is most convenient to remove either
X_{1}
or
X_{2}
, so let us choose to eliminate
X_{1}
and consider the evolution of the system
\{X_{2},Z\}
described by the dynamical equations

\displaystyle\frac{dX_{2}}{dN}

\displaystyle=-\frac{X_{2}\left((Z-1)^{2}f+2H_{0}^{2}Z\left(X_{2}(Z-1)+2(Z-1)f^{\prime}+48H_{0}^{2}Zf^{\prime\prime}\right)\right)}{2H_{0}^{2}Z\big((1-Z)f^{\prime}+12H_{0}^{2}Zf^{\prime\prime}\big)}\,,

(6.2.6)

\displaystyle\frac{dZ}{dN}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(Z-1)^{2}\left((Z-1)f+2H_{0}^{2}Z(X_{2}+6f^{\prime})\right)}{2H_{0}^{2}\big((1-Z)f^{\prime}+12H_{0}^{2}Zf^{\prime\prime}\big)}\,,

(6.2.7)

where
N=\log a(t)
. The acceleration equation (
6.2.3
) and Friedmann constraint (
6.2.5
) have both been used to remove
\dot{H}
terms and
X_{1}
terms respectively. The system is then closed when a function
f
has been specified.
Note that
f
has dimensions
H_{0}^{2}
,
f^{\prime}
is dimensionless and
f^{\prime\prime}
has units of
H_{0}^{-2}
. Consequently, equations (
6.2.6
)–(
6.2.7
) are dimensionless.

The system (
6.2.6
)–(
6.2.7
) can be written more compactly by introducing functions related to
f
and its derivatives

\displaystyle m(Z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2Qf^{\prime\prime}(Q)}{f^{\prime}(Q)}=\frac{12H_{0}^{2}Zf^{\prime\prime}}{(1-Z)f^{\prime}}\,,

(6.2.8)

\displaystyle n(Z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{f(Q)}{Qf^{\prime}(Q)}=\frac{f(1-Z)}{6H_{0}^{2}Zf^{\prime}}\,,

(6.2.9)

where the functions on the right-hand side should again be treated as
f({6H_{0}^{2}Z}/{(1-Z)})
.
The dynamical equations can then be brought into the following form

\displaystyle X_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{2}\left(2-3n-4m+X_{2}/f^{\prime}\right)}{1+m}\,,

(6.2.10)

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{Z(Z-1)\left(X_{2}-3(n-2)f^{\prime}\right)}{(1+m)f^{\prime}}\ .

(6.2.11)

It turns out that many properties of this system and its critical points are independent of
f
, and hence are valid for all models. This makes our approach very broad, as will be demonstrated below.

6.2.1
Fixed points

The fixed points of the system (
6.2.10
)-(
6.2.11
) can be divided into two families of solutions: one with
X_{2}=0
, and the other with
X_{2}=(4m+3n-2)f^{\prime}=:X_{2}^{*}
. For the case where
X_{2}=0
, the points with
Z=Z^{*}
are solutions of the algebraic equation

\displaystyle\frac{3Z(Z-1)(n(Z)-2)}{1+m(Z)}=0\ .

(6.2.12)

For the
X_{2}=X_{2}^{*}
case, the
Z
coordinates take values
Z=0
or
Z=1
. The
X_{2}
coordinates must then be evaluated at
Z=0
or
Z=1
because
X_{2}^{*}
is function of
Z
.

These results have been collected in Table
6.1
. The points A and B are solutions with
X_{2}=X_{2}^{*}
evaluated at
Z=0
and
Z=1
respectively. The P’s represent points along the
X_{2}=0
line with
Z=Z^{*}
satisfying (
6.2.12
). The special cases of
Z^{*}
when
n(Z)=2
and
m(Z)\rightarrow\infty
are labelled as P
n
and P
m
respectively. Note that
m(Z)
can diverge when
f^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
. However,
f^{\prime}
appears in the denominator of
n(Z)
(
6.2.9
) and in both dynamical equations (
6.2.10
) and (
6.2.11
). Therefore for
m(Z)\rightarrow\infty
to correspond to a critical point, we must also require
n(Z)
to remain finite. In other words, we require that
f^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\infty
or that both
f\rightarrow 0
and
f^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
, for some value(s) of
Z
. For further details, see
[
223
]
.

Table 6.1
:
Table of fixed points of system (
6.2.10
)–(
6.2.11
) for arbitrary function
f
. For existence, we assume that all variables and functions are finite at the critical point unless otherwise stated.

The system also displays divergent behaviours along ‘singular lines’. The line L1 exists for values
Z=Z_{c}
such that
m(Z_{c})=-1
, while L2 exists if
n(Z)\rightarrow\infty
with
m(Z)
finite. This means that either
f\rightarrow\infty
or that both
f^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
and
f^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0
such that
n(Z)
diverges but
m(Z)
does not, similar to the conditions for P
m
.

The corresponding values of
X_{1}
determined by the Friedmann constraint have also been included in Table
6.1
. The final column states the specific conditions for each point, and it is assumed that all variables
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
,
Z
must be finite for existence. Note that the fixed points are not necessarily unique and in many cases they coincide with one another. Moreover, ‘asymptotic points’ (e.g., at
X_{2}\rightarrow\infty
) are not captured by the above analysis due to the assumptions of finiteness. A key example where this type of critical point exists will be shown in Sec.
6.2.2
. For compact
f(T)
or
f(Q)
models, however, all critical points can simply be read off from the above table.

In Appendix
E.1
we provide a detailed stability analysis for the critical points of the general system. The de Sitter point P
n
is found to be stable, with negative eigenvalues (
E.1.4
). For the other points, the stability depends on the specific form of the function
f
and are model-dependent.

Physical Parameters

Using the formulation above (
6.2.8
)–(
6.2.11
) along with the cosmological field equations, the deceleration parameter can be expressed in terms of the new variables

\displaystyle q:=-\frac{\ddot{a}a}{\dot{a}^{2}}=-1-\frac{\dot{H}}{H^{2}}=-1-\frac{(\frac{3}{2}X_{1}+2X_{2})(n-2)}{(X_{1}+X_{2})(1+m)}\,.

(6.2.13)

The value of
q
for each of the fixed points is given in Table
6.2
. For the points P
n
, P
m
, A and B one obtains fixed values of
q
, which are model-independent. The points P
1
and P
2
depend on the functions
m
and
n
evaluated at their respective
Z
values, and so a specific
f
is needed to determine
q
. For the lines L1 and L2, the divergent behaviour of the system makes any concrete claims about physical parameters untrustworthy, so they have not been included.

The effective equation of state parameter
w_{\textrm{eff}}
can be expressed in a similar form

\displaystyle w_{\textrm{eff}}:=\frac{p_{\textrm{tot}}}{\rho_{\textrm{tot}}}=-1+\frac{(X_{1}+\frac{4}{3}X_{2})(n-2)}{(X_{1}+X_{2})(1+m)}\ ,

(6.2.14)

where the total energy density is
\rho_{\textrm{tot}}=\rho_{m}+\rho_{r}+\rho_{f}
and
\rho_{f}
represents the extra terms in (
6.2.2
) that do not appear in the standard Friedmann equation of GR

\rho_{f}:=3H^{2}+\frac{1}{2}f-6H^{2}f^{\prime}\,.

(6.2.15)

Similarly, the total pressure is
p_{\textrm{tot}}=p_{m}+p_{r}+p_{f}
with
p_{f}
representing the additional terms in the acceleration equation (
6.2.3
). The values of
w_{\textrm{eff}}
evaluated at the critical points are also given in Table
6.2
. Lastly, the values of the Hubble parameter
H(t)
are also included, provided they are well-defined.

\displaystyle-\frac{-1+m(1)+n(1)}{1+m(1)}
